So, the focus stuff has kind of been there for a while.\n\nDoctor: Looking back before you turned 12, did you ever have trouble staying still or find yourself acting without thinking, such as blurting out answers or interrupting others?\n\nPatient: No, like I kind of just mentioned, I didn't really have issues with staying still or acting without thinking. I wasn't the type of kid to blurt out answers or interrupt people. I was usually pretty quiet... just, you know, not really focused on the right things. But moving around or speaking out of turn wasn't a problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do you ever find yourself having trouble staying focused or being easily distracted, such as when you’re at home doing chores or trying to relax?\n\nPatient: No, not really. When I'm at home, I don't really notice any issues with focusing or getting distracted. If I'm doing chores or just relaxing, I seem to get through it fine. It's really just work where I'm struggling to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice these focus issues or the need to get up from your seat in other settings, like when you're out running errands, socializing, or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed it in those situations either. When I'm out running errands or hanging out with friends, or even just doing a hobby, I don't feel like I'm losing focus. And I definitely don't feel the need to get up or move around. Like I said, it really just seems to be a problem when I'm at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to stay focused or feel restless during structured activities outside of work, like when you're working on a hobby, doing errands, or following a routine task?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, I really don't have those issues outside of work. It's really just when I'm at work that I struggle with this stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. When I'm at home, I don't really notice any issues with focusing or getting distracted. If I'm doing chores or just relaxing, I seem to get through it fine. It's really just work where I'm struggling to stay on track.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever notice these focus issues or the need to get up from your seat in other settings, like when you're out running errands, socializing, or doing something you enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed it in those situations either. When I'm out running errands or hanging out with friends, or even just doing a hobby, I don't feel like I'm losing focus. And I definitely don't feel the need to get up or move around. Like I said, it really just seems to be a problem when I'm at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to stay focused or feel restless during structured activities outside of work, like when you're working on a hobby, doing errands, or following a routine task?\n\nPatient: No, like I was just saying, I really don't have those issues outside of work. When I'm doing a hobby or running errands, I'm fine. I don't feel restless or have trouble paying attention to what I'm doing. It's really just when I'm at work that I struggle with this stuff.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any situation outside of work—like when you're studying, reading, or working on a project at home—where you've had trouble staying focused or felt the need to get up from your seat?\n\nPatient: Honestly, no. I don't take any classes right now or volunteer anywhere. And if I go to a long event, like a game or a movie or something, I'm usually there because I want to be, so I don't really have an issue paying attention or sitting there. It really just seems to be my job right now, and, well, school back in the day.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you think of any other settings—like when you're volunteering, taking a class, or attending a long event—where you've had trouble staying focused or felt the need to get up, even if it wasn't at work or school?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / insurance_claims_or_policy_processing_clerk / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
